Analytical solutions and chaotic insights into the Hirota-Maccari system
Authors: Tarmizi Usman, Mohammad Safi Ullah
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
This article discusses the (2 + 1)-dimensional Hirota-Maccari (HM) model, a particular type of Schrödinger equation that addresses various nonlinear phenomena in physics, optics, fluid dynamics, plasma physics, and other scientific areas. It uses a variable relation to transform the system into an ordinary form and builds different soliton solutions using the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( {\frac{{G^{\prime } }}{{G^{2} }}} \right)$$\end{document}-expansion and generalized \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( {\frac{{G^{\prime } }}{G}} \right)$$\end{document}-expansion approaches. We create double periodic waves, dark solitons, bright solitons, anti-compacton solitons, bright dark breather waves, periodic multiple waves, multiple dark-bright breather waves, compactons, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W$$\end{document}-shaped periodic waves using the above methods plus a soft computing package. We numerically simulate some results in 3D with density, 2D, and contour formats. Additionally, we converted the dynamic planner structure of the governing model using the Galilean transformation. We then studied the chaotic properties of this model using various chaos-detecting tools, including fractal dimensions, basins of attraction, recurrence maps, strange attractors, multistability, and return maps. The significance of this study lies in its ability to bridge the theoretical understanding of the governing model with potential applications in diverse nonlinear physical systems. To our knowledge, these two methods have not yet yielded any solutions to the underlying model.

Introduction

Background

In recent decades, researchers have used nonlinear models to simulate different physical processes and phenomena1,2. Over the past few years, diverse domains, such as electromagnetic wave fields3, shallow water waves4, quantum mechanics5, plasma physics6, condensed matter physics7, fluid dynamics8, Heat transfer9, oceanography10, optical fiber communications11, and others have applied the dynamics of nonlinear waves. As the telecommunications sector develops, many nonlinear models, like the Klein–Gordan system12, the KDV–KP equation13, the Kopel triopoly model14, the Whitham–Broer–Kaup dynamical equation15, the KFG equation16, the Hamiltonian amplitude model17, Kundu–Mukherjee–Naskar model18, Schrödinger–Hirota equation19, Boussinesq model20, and others, appear in the context of nonlinear processes. Since problems representing physical processes have led to significant discoveries that greatly impact life, it is essential to develop accurate solutions. Therefore, mathematical research employs various approaches, like the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( {\frac{1}{{G^{\prime } }}} \right)$$\end{document}-technique21, the simplest equation process22, the modified extended tanh-function scheme23, the unified solver technique24, the modified simple equation method25, the extended Jacobian elliptic function expansion process26, the modified Sardar sub-equation procedure27, the reductive perturbation technique28, the improved \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F$$\end{document}-expansion method29,30, and others for solving nonlinear models.

Literature review

Bifurcation, sensitivity study, and exact traveling wave outcomes of the governing model were provided by Han and Zhao in 202331. Ismael and others obtained optical soliton outcomes of the adopted model by the modified auxiliary equation method in 202432. Akram and Tang studied the bifurcation and stochastic dynamics of the stated model in 202533. The Soliton solution of this model’s stochastic form was subjected to multiplicative white noise according to the Itô sense using the generalized Jacobi elliptic function method by Alngar et al. in 202534.

Advantages of chaotic and bifurcation analysis

Bifurcation and chaotic analysis are crucial for understanding nonlinear dynamical systems. Chaotic analysis focuses on sensitivity to initial conditions and long-term unpredictability, as well as the potential for intricate spatiotemporal patterns. Meanwhile, bifurcation analysis helps to identify qualitative changes in system behavior when parameters change. These analyses are commonly used in applied mathematics, physics, and engineering for stability assessment, control design, and predicting emergent dynamics35.

Existing gaps

The previously stated works demonstrate that the Hirota-Maccari equation has been extensively discussed. Despite its relevance, the unified analytical and numerical analyses of the Hirota-Macari system remain unpublished. It has been challenging to integrate both perspectives, as most existing studies have focused on exact solutions or soliton behaviors, such as periodic and breather waves. Additionally, perturbations and parameter sensitivity have not been comprehensively investigated in the transition from regular to chaotic regimes. Furthermore, the topics of various tools for identifying chaos, such as fractal dimensions, basins of attraction, bifurcation diagrams, return maps, recurrence plots, multistability, Lyapunov exponents, and strange attractor designs, remain unexplored for the suggested model.

Motivation and justification of the chosen expansion method

The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( {\frac{{G^{\prime } }}{{G^{2} }}} \right)$$\end{document} expansion and the generalized \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( {\frac{{G^{\prime } }}{G}} \right)$$\end{document}-expansion methods are employed in this work because they can solve various nonlinear equations, particularly those with intricate nonlinear models, such as the Hirota-Maccari model. These two methods create various exact and analytical solutions, including double periodic waves, dark solitons, bright solitons, anti-compacton solitons, bright-dark breather waves, multiple periodic waves, multiple dark-bright breather waves, compactons, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W$$\end{document}-shaped periodic waves, which play a significant role in the system’s dynamics. Their versatility in managing intricate nonlinearities is one of their key benefits, which makes them applicable to diverse issues. Compared to many other approaches, they are also straightforward, easy to use, and provide a clear physical grasp of the answers. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( {\frac{{G^{\prime } }}{{G^{2} }}} \right)$$\end{document} expansion and generalized \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( {\frac{{G^{\prime } }}{G}} \right)$$\end{document}-expansion methods’ applicability is limited to various nonlinear equation types, and their complexity increases when dealing with multivariable or higher-dimensional systems. Furthermore, the choice of beginning assumptions or trial functions may impact the method’s efficacy. Notwithstanding these drawbacks, these expansion schemes are still effective methods of obtaining precise answers to complicated nonlinear systems. Based on this motivation, we consider these two expansion methods for finding soliton solutions of the governing model.

New contributions of the study

By combining analytical methods with detailed dynamical analysis, this study aims to fill these gaps. To reach our goals, we comprehensively analyze the governing model using two analytical methods and extract the equation’s exact solutions. Additionally, we will use phase portraits, multistability, and various tools to identify chaos in the dynamical system. The originality of our work lies in our use of the indicated techniques to conclude Eq. (1), which no other researcher has previously documented.

Graphical abstract.

The underlying nonlinear problem

The Hirota-Maccari equation is a commonly used model for describing several issues in different domains, including fluid dynamics, optical fiber communication, plasma physics, and others36. Nonlinear optics explains how polarized light pulses move through special fibers and waveguides with different properties in different directions. It models the interplay between ion-acoustic waves and fluctuations in plasma density in plasma physics. The model also applies to fluid dynamics, effectively capturing stratified fluids’ nonlinear surface and internal wave behavior. Simulation of realistic waves on multiple scales is possible through these diverse applications. The nonlinear Hirota-Maccari structure can be stated in the following manner371\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i{w}_{t}+{w}_{xy}+i{w}_{xxx}+wv-i{\left|w\right|}^{2}{w}_{x}=0, 3{v}_{t}+{\left({\left|w\right|}^{2}\right)}_{y}=0,$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v$$\end{document} signifies a real scalar field, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w$$\end{document} denotes a complex scalar field for the time component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t$$\end{document} and distance components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y$$\end{document}.

Results and discussions

Graphical presentation

For the visual presentation, we see that outcome \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${v}_{8}$$\end{document} represents bright soliton as exposed in Fig. 2 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=1$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b=$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r=2$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${l}_{-1}=$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=N=M=1$$\end{document} at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y=0$$\end{document}. Then, we see that the outcome \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${w}_{8}$$\end{document} represents double periodic wave and dark soliton as visible in Fig. 3 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=1$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b=r=2$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${l}_{-1}=k=N=M=1$$\end{document} at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y=0$$\end{document}. Physical interpretation of the findings, their applications, and limitations

We create numerous periodic waves, dark solitons, bright solitons, anti-compacton solitons, breather waves, and compactons. This section describes their applications and limitations. A periodic wave is a continuous, repeating pattern that propagates infinitely in time, space, or both17. Periodic waves with two independent directions are called double periodic waves. A periodic wave is applied to models repeating patterns in fluids, optics, and plasma physics. The main limitation of this wave is that it is a non-localized wave and carries infinite energy in unbounded domains. It is not confined to space and may become unstable under certain conditions. Not all real waves are truly periodic. A bright soliton is a localized peak that travels through a nonlinear medium without distortion23. It is applied in optics, plasmas, and Bose–Einstein condensates, among other areas. It may become unstable under certain interactions, be sensitive to perturbations, and only form in the presence of focusing nonlinearity. A dark soliton is a localized dip in a continuous wave background that travels without distortion23. It is applied in optics, plasmas, and Bose–Einstein condensates, among other areas. It requires a non-zero background, is not suitable for focusing on nonlinearity, and is sensitive to perturbations. A breather wave is a localized wave that holds a periodic or quasi-periodic pattern in a certain area42. If it contains both bright and dark waves, then it is called a bright-dark breather wave. A breather wave is applied in fluids, optics, and Bose–Einstein condensates. It is unstable in real systems. It can qualitatively evolve into soliton or chaotic states. It requires specific conditions to form. A compacton is a soliton-like wave with compact support43. It is applied in traffic flow, population dynamics, or biophysics to describe humps or density accumulations. Require special nonlinearity and are less commonly observed physically. An anti-compacton is the negative-amplitude counterpart of a compacton43. It is applied in traffic flow, population dynamics, or biophysics to describe troughs, voids, or density reductions. Require special nonlinearity and are difficult to observe physically.

Chaotic phenomena with various chaos-identification tools

This segment employs disturbed components to inspect the chaotic nature42,44 of the upcoming dynamical structure. This examination describes two- and three-dimensional phase diagrams. By introducing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{dW}{d\varsigma }=S$$\end{document}, we obtain the subsequent dynamical form of Eq. (3):16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{dW}{d\varsigma }=S, \frac{dS}{d\varsigma }=mW+n{W}^{3},$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m=\frac{{a}^{3}-ab-r}{1-3a}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=\frac{\left(3a-1\right)}{3\left(1-3a\right)}$$\end{document}. Now, we include a disturbed component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Acos(\mu \varsigma )$$\end{document} in Eq. (16), then one reaches the next form:17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{dW}{d\varsigma }=S, \frac{dS}{d\varsigma }=mW+n{W}^{3}+Acos\left(\mu \varsigma \right),$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document} denote the perturbation’s strength and frequency, respectively. An effective method for examining stability and chaos in dynamical structures is the numerical description of Lyapunov exponents. Nevertheless, achieving success requires extensive knowledge of dynamics and a precise understanding of data. The Lyapunov exponent is a crucial tool for identifying chaos in a system. Adjacent orbits show exponential divergences, indicative of chaos when the exponent is positive. If the exponent is negative, it indicates stability, as neighboring trajectories exhibit rapid convergence. Calculating such exponential notions is undoubtedly necessary to comprehend nonlinear dynamics. With appropriate selections, we derive the system’s Lyapunov exponents. Figure 16 illustrates the system’s complex, chaotic structure with a positive Lyapunov exponent. A brief description of the Lyapunov exponent iterative process.

Figure 17 depicts the bifurcation plot of the structure Eq. (17) focusing on three parameters: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A$$\end{document}. We can observe that Fig. 17a displays a stable nature when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m$$\end{document} is negative and a chaotic nature when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m$$\end{document} is positive. Figure 17b shows the chaotic nature of both the negative and positive values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m$$\end{document}. For small values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A$$\end{document}, Fig. 17c represents stable behavior, but for large values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A$$\end{document}, it moves toward chaotic behavior. Comparison with previous literature

This section highlights the uniqueness and achievements of this study. We can reach our destination by combining our outcomes with those of the previously stated works in45–50. Yu et al. investigated the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N$$\end{document}-soliton solution of the suggested problem by utilizing the Hirota bilinear approach45. Through the Jacobi elliptic function technique, Tarla and his research team developed the governing model’s dark-bright and dark soliton solutions46. Bulut and his collaborators obtain the solitary and topological solitons of the derived model through the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m + \left( {\frac{1}{{G^{\prime } }}} \right)$$\end{document}-expansion scheme47. Günay extracts some exact outcomes to the stated nonlinear problem via the generalized exponential rational function scheme48. Maccari analyzes the parametric character of the proposed nonlinear structure by applying the asymptotic perturbation scheme49. Demiray et al. investigated the exact responses of the proposed model by using the extended trial equation process50. Our research yielded outcomes for double periodic waves, dark solitons, bright solitons, anti-compacton solitons, bright dark breather waves, periodic multiple waves, multiple bright-dark breather waves, compactons, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W$$\end{document}-shaped periodic waves. Additionally, we investigate the chaotic nature with various chaos identification tools. When we compare our outcomes to formerly reported work in45–50, some of our outcomes differ from theirs.

Conclusion

Summary of the findings

This article successfully studies the (2 + 1)-dimensional HM model, a unique nonlinear Schrödinger equation dealing with various nonlinear properties in physics, optics, and other scientific areas. We change the system into an ordinary differential form using a variable relation. Then, we use the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( {\frac{{G^{\prime } }}{{G^{2} }}} \right)$$\end{document}-expansion and generalized \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( {\frac{{G^{\prime } }}{G}} \right)$$\end{document}-expansion processes to find several soliton solutions. Combining the mentioned techniques with a soft computing program generates double periodic waves, dark solitons, bright solitons, anti-compacton solitons, bright dark breather waves, periodic multiple waves, multiple bright-dark breather waves, compactons, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W$$\end{document}-shaped periodic waves. We numerically simulate some outcomes in 3D with density, 2D, and contour versions. Furthermore, we applied the Galilean transformation to the governing model’s dynamic planner structure. Next, we used different chaos-detecting tools like strange attractors, basins of attraction, fractal dimensions, multistability, and recurrence plots, to examine this model’s chaotic features. This study offers valuable insights into Hirota-Maccari dynamics by integrating exact solutions with chaotic analysis, which gives future research directions. To our knowledge, these two approaches have not yet solved the underlying model.

Limitations of the study

This study focuses on the (2 + 1)-dimensional nonlinear problem with constant coefficients. We do not consider multidimensional extensions, variable coefficients, or fractional derivatives. Additionally, we qualitatively present the physical applicability of the solutions, but not practically or numerically.

Future work

Using soliton solutions, multistability analysis, chaotic dynamics, and diverse chaos-detection tools, we have investigated the Hirota-Maccari equation in this work. While the results enhance our understanding of nonlinear dynamics, there are numerous opportunities for further development of this study. Future research may expand on this topic in many fascinating ways. The Hirota-Maccari equation effectively understands wave motion in intricate real-world systems, especially when random disturbances and weird diffusion occur. Further research might confirm the theoretical conclusions using numerical simulations and experimental settings, even though this work emphasizes analytical approaches. These future initiatives will improve the effect and usefulness of this study in several scientific and technical domains.
